./research_state/campaign-10-ultra/superseded/pre-d-101/submissions/researcher-0001/response.json:171:  "note_markdown": "# The zero-degree endpoint is false, and the KTZ cubic loss is a sparsifier\u2013interpolation\u2013root-budget bottleneck\n\n## Abstract\n\nFor the affine line-versus-point test on $F_p^2$, the literal parameter range $0\u2264d<p$ is unsound at $d=0$. For every integer $r\u22652$ and every prime\n\n$p\u2265p_0(r):=max\\{30,r(r-1),8r!\\}$,\n\nthere is a table $f:F_p^2\u2192F_p$ and a constant polynomial on every line such that the local agreement $\u03b5$ is at least $r/(130p)$, whereas every global constant agrees with $f$ on at most $2/p$ of the plane. Thus the global/local ratio is at most $260/r$, and no absolute $\u03a9(\u03b5)$ conclusion holds at the zero threshold $C(0)^{1/3}$.\n\nFor the corrected regime $1\u2264d<p$, I find no flaw in the bivariate Kominers\u2013Thaler\u2013Zheng proof and no improved exponent. Its cubic loss is exact: sparsification gives $|R|=O(p/\u03b5)$; weighted interpolation gives $D^2\u2248d|R|$; extension, deletion, and lifting independently require $D=O(\u03b5p)$. Hence $\u03b5^3\u2273d/p$. This is my exponent reconstruction, not a separately quoted KTZ theorem.\n\nI also prove two positive-degree obstructions. First, density and minimum degree alone may require $\u03a9(p/\u03b5)$ reaching lines, so the KTZ sparsifier scale cannot be improved by a purely incidence-theoretic lemma. Second, a random interpolation construction has local agreement $\u0398(d/p)$ but vanishing global/local ratio along $d\u2248sqrt(p/log p)$, confirming that $d/p$ is the correct long-term scale up to constants.\n\n## Test and Notation\n\nFix a prime $p$, dimension $m=2$, and $0\u2264d<p$. Let $L$ be the set of the $p(p+1)$ geometric affine lines in $F_p^2$. Fix an affine bijection $ell_L:F_p\u2192L$ for each line. A line table assigns $P_L\u2208F_p[t]$ with $deg(P_L)\u2264d$.\n\nThe local agreement is\n\n$Acc(f,P):=Pr_{L\u2208L,\\ t\u2208F_p}[P_L(t)=f(ell_L(t))]=:\u03b5$.\n\nEvery line has $p$ points and every point lies on $p+1$ lines, so uniform line followed by uniform point is exactly uniform sampling from incident point-line pairs. The global agreement is\n\n$Agr_d(f):=max_{Q\u2208F_p[X,Y],\\ totaldeg(Q)\u2264d} Pr_x[Q(x)=f(x)]$.\n\nWrite $\u03c1=d/p$. Because $0<\u03c1<1$ when $d>0$, a larger threshold exponent is stronger.\n\n## Prior Results\n\nThe source-stated result is [Kominers\u2013Thaler\u2013Zheng, ECCC TR26-147, Revision 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/147/revision/1/download); the [ECCC record](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/147/) dates Revision 1 to August 16, 2026. Specializing Theorem 1.1 to $q=p$ and $m=2$, it states that absolute constants $C,c>0$ exist such that\n\n$\u03b5\u2265C(d/p)^{1/3}$ implies $Agr_d(f)\u2265c\u03b5$.\n\nSection 6 supplies $c=1/8000$ in the bivariate proof. Revision 1 leaves $C$ existential. The ignored local cache `.cache/references/ktz.pdf` is the earlier 17-page version, SHA-256 prefix `f8c5b145`, and must not be used to attribute its explicit $C=10^{40}$ assertion to Revision 1.\n\nThe exact dependency map is KTZ Lemma 2.1 for incidence mixing; Lemmas 2.2\u20132.4 for characteristic-compatible interpolation, squarefreeness, and a coprime directional derivative; Lemmas 2.5\u20132.7 for line counting, the resultant bound, and simple-root lifting; Lemmas 3.1\u20133.2 for pruning and sparsification; Lemmas 4.1\u20134.3 for the explainer; Lemmas 5.1\u20135.2 for removal of degenerate roots; and Lemmas 6.1\u20136.3 for lifting and propagation. KTZ's Lemma 2.7 is traced there to [HKSS, Lemma 2.10](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.12752); Lemma 6.1 is essentially the corresponding HKSS local lifting step. No Arora\u2013Sudan black box is used in this audit.\n\nThe corpus contains no prior mathematical submission or verifier audit: all eight leaderboard files are `[]`, neither campaign database contains submission or audit tables, and no submission or review directory exists. Thus no campaign-verified result can be imported.\n\n## Theorem\n\n### Theorem A \u2014 zero-degree endpoint counterexample\n\nFor every integer $r\u22652$ and every prime $p\u2265p_0(r)$, there exist $f:F_p^2\u2192F_p$ and constant line polynomials $P_L$ such that\n\n$Acc(f,P)\u2265r/(130p)$ and $Agr_0(f)\u22642/p$.\n\nConsequently, for every proposed absolute $c>0$, choosing $r>260/c$ gives $Agr_0(f)<c Acc(f,P)$. Since $C(d/p)^{1/3}=0$ at $d=0$, the theorem requested over the literal range $0\u2264d<p$ is false.\n\n### Proposition B \u2014 exact positive-degree cubic bottleneck\n\nAssume $1\u2264d<p$ and let $\u03b5=Acc(f,P)$. KTZ constructs\n\n$k_0=floor(\u03b5p/100)$, $100p\u2264r:=|R|\u22646400p/\u03b5$,\n\n$D=16(floor(sqrt(dr))+1)\u22641280(sqrt(dp/\u03b5)+1)$,\n\nand $k_1=floor(\u03b5p/1000)$.\n\nThe three requirements $D<k_0/2$, $D/p=O(\u03b5)$, and $D<k_1/2$ all reduce to $\u03b5^3\u2273d/p$. The exceptional-line term $D^2/[p(p+1)]$ asks only $\u03b5^2\u2273d/p$. Once these inequalities hold, KTZ obtains $Agr_d(f)\u2265\u03b5/8000$.\n\n### Theorem C \u2014 incidence-only reaching-set obstruction\n\nLet $0<\u03b4\u22641/4$ and let $p$ be prime with\n\n$\u03b4^2p\u226564 log(256ep)$.\n\nThere exists an accepted-incidence subgraph $G$ of the affine plane such that every point and line has degree at least $\u03b4p/2$, every line has degree at most $2\u03b4p$, yet no set $R$ of at most $p/(64\u03b4)$ lines satisfies\n\n$cov_R(L):=|\\{x\u2208L:(L,x)\u2208E(G),\\text{ and some }M\u2208R\\text{ has }(M,x)\u2208E(G)\\}|>\u03b4p/4$\n\nfor every $L$ outside $R$.\n\nMoreover, $G$ is exactly realizable as a line-versus-point acceptance graph with $f=0$ and degree\n\n$d_G:=max_L deg_G(L)\u22642\u03b4p<p$.\n\nIf its local agreement is $\u03b1$, then $\u03b1\u2208[\u03b4/2,2\u03b4]$, and every such reaching family has size greater than $p/(128\u03b1)$.\n\n### Proposition D \u2014 a positive-degree sharp-scale adversary\n\nFor every prime $p\u22653$ and $1\u2264d<p$, put\n\n$K=(d+1)(d+2)/2$, $T=ceil(4(p+K log p))$.\n\nThere exist $f$ and degree-$d$ line polynomials satisfying\n\n$(d+1)/p\u2264Acc(f,P)\u22644(d+2)/p$\n\nand\n\n$Agr_d(f)\u2264T/p^2$.\n\nAlong $d=floor(sqrt(p/log p))$, this gives $Agr_d(f)=O(1/p)$ and $Agr_d(f)/Acc(f,P)=O(1/d)\u21920$.\n\n## Proof or Conditional Proof\n\n**[P1 \u2014 proved] Sampling and source specialization.** There are $p(p+1)$ affine lines, $p$ points per line, and $p+1$ lines through each point. Hence both descriptions of the sampling distribution assign mass $1/[p^2(p+1)]$ to every incident pair. KTZ Theorem 1.1 therefore specializes without a sampling change.\n\n**[P2 \u2014 proved] KTZ sparsifier scale.** KTZ Lemma 3.1 produces an accepted graph $G_0$ of edge measure at least $\u03b5/4$ and minimum degree $k_0=floor(\u03b5p/100)$. Lemma 3.2 chooses a line family $R$ with normalized line measure between $100/p$ and $3200/(\u03b5p)$ and covers more than $k_0/2$ accepted points of every retained line. Since the line space has $p(p+1)$ elements, this gives $100p\u2264|R|\u22646400p/\u03b5$.\n\n**[P3 \u2014 proved] Weighted interpolation scale.** KTZ gives $dim(V_D)\u2265D^3/(64d)$ for monomials of weight $(1,1,d)$. On a selected line, $A(ell_L(t),P_L(t))$ has degree at most $D$, so its formal vanishing imposes at most $D+1$ linear constraints. A nonzero interpolant therefore requires $D^3/d$ to dominate $|R|D$, or $D^2\u2273d|R|$. The source chooses $D=16(floor(sqrt(d|R|))+1)$, yielding the displayed bound. This coefficient count is an exponent reconstruction from KTZ's parameters.\n\n**[P4 \u2014 proved] The cubic coupling.** Dividing the displayed upper bound by $\u03b5p$ gives\n\n$D/(\u03b5p)\u22641280(sqrt(\u03c1/\u03b5^3)+1/(\u03b5p))$.\n\nLemma 4.3 needs $D<k_0/2$ to turn more than $D$ distinct covered roots on a line into a formal identity. Section 5 loses normalized incidence mass\n\n$D/p+D^2/[p(p+1)]$,\n\nwhose first term must be $O(\u03b5)$. Lemma 6.1 again needs $D<k_1/2$ to obtain more than $D$ incident line roots. Each dominant requirement therefore asks $\u03c1/\u03b5^3=O(1)$, namely $\u03b5\u2273\u03c1^{1/3}$. By contrast, the quadratic deletion term is $O(\u03c1/\u03b5)$ and asks only $\u03c1=O(\u03b5^2)$.\n\n**[P5 \u2014 proved] Probability and constant-loss audit.** In the source bookkeeping, popularity averaging charges at most $\u03b5/2$ to low-popularity incidences; the first degree pruning charges less than $\u03b5/50$ and retains at least $\u03b5/4$. Lemma 3.2 uses a Chernoff bound for each line's coverage, a union bound over $p(p+1)$ lines, and a second-moment/Chebyshev bound for $|R|$; its parameter lemma ensures $\u03b5p\u226blog p$. Section 5 charges exactly $D/p+D^2/[p(p+1)]\u2264\u03b5/16$. The second pruning charges less than $\u03b5/16$ and leaves edge measure at least $\u03b5/8$. Lemma 2.1 is the sole incidence Cauchy\u2013Schwarz loss, with normalized error at most $sqrt(\u03bc(X)\u03bc(T)/(p+1))$; the imposed $p^{-1/2}\u2264\u03b5/8000$ yields component measures at least $\u03b5/8000$. There is no additional Markov loss in KTZ. Thus no suppressed power of $\u03c1$ occurs after [P4].\n\n**[P6 \u2014 proved] A good line at d=0.** Choose $f(x)$ independently and uniformly from $F_p$. On a fixed line let $N_a$ be the occupancy of color $a$ and let $Y=sum_a 1[N_a=r]$. With\n\n$a_0=Pr[N_a=r]=binom(p,r)p^{-r}(1-1/p)^{p-r}$,\n\nthe assumptions on $p$ give\n\n$prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(1-i/p)\u22651-r(r-1)/(2p)\u22651/2$\n\nand $(1-1/p)^{p-r}\u2265(1-1/p)^p\u22651/4$. Hence $a_0\u22651/(8r!)$ and $E[Y]=pa_0\u22651$. For distinct colors,\n\n$Pr[N_a=N_b=r]\u22641/(r!)^2\u226464a_0^2$.\n\nTherefore $E[Y^2]\u2264E[Y]+64E[Y]^2\u226465E[Y]^2$. The second-moment inequality gives $Pr[Y>0]\u22651/65$.\n\n**[P7 \u2014 proved] Simultaneous global balance.** Let $g(f)$ be the fraction of lines with $Y>0$, so $E[g]\u22651/65$. For a fixed color, its total occupancy in the plane is $Bin(p^2,1/p)$ with mean $p$. Chernoff and a union bound over all $p$ colors give\n\n$Pr[max_a |f^{-1}(a)|>2p]\u2264p(e/4)^p\u2264pe^{-p/3}\u22641/130$.\n\nIf $H$ is the complementary event, then $E[g1_H]\u22651/65-1/130=1/130$. Thus some $f$ satisfies $H$ and $g(f)\u22651/130$. On each good line choose a color occurring exactly $r$ times and use it as the constant $P_L$; label other lines arbitrarily. This gives $Acc\u2265r/(130p)$, while $H$ gives $Agr_0\u22642/p$. Independence between different lines was never assumed.\n\n**[P8 \u2014 refuted] Universal soundness over 0\u2264d<p.** If an absolute conclusion $Agr_0\u2265c Acc$ held, [P7] would imply $c\u2264260/r$ for every $r$. Choosing $r>260/c$ is a contradiction. The threshold constant $C$ cannot repair this because its threshold is identically zero at $d=0$.\n\n**[P9 \u2014 proved] Random incidence graph.** Retain every affine point-line incidence independently with probability $\u03b4$. Chernoff bounds and a union bound over fewer than $3p^2$ point and line vertices show, with probability greater than $3/4$ under the stated hypothesis, that every point and line has degree at least $\u03b4p/2$ and every line has degree at most $2\u03b4p$.\n\n**[P10 \u2014 proved] No small reaching family.** Fix $R$ with $r\u2264r_0=floor(p/(64\u03b4))$. Some parallel class contains at most $r/(p+1)$ lines of $R$, leaving at least $p/2$ test lines outside $R$. For such a line $L$, let $n_x$ count lines of $R$ through $x\u2208L$. Then $sum_x n_x\u2264r$. The coverage indicators at distinct $x$ are independent and\n\n$E[cov_R(L)]\u2264\u03b4 sum_x[1-(1-\u03b4)^{n_x}]\u2264\u03b4^2r\u2264\u03b4p/64$.\n\nConsequently\n\n$Pr[cov_R(L)>\u03b4p/4]\u2264(e/16)^{\u03b4p/4}$.\n\nFor distinct parallel test lines the relevant incidence variables are disjoint, so the probability that this fixed $R$ reaches all of them is at most $exp(-\u03b4p^2/32)$. The number of sets of at most $r_0$ lines is at most\n\n$exp((p/(64\u03b4))log(256ep))\u2264exp(\u03b4p^2/4096)$.\n\nA union bound gives probability less than $1/4$ that any small $R$ succeeds. Intersecting with [P9] proves Theorem C.\n\n**[P11 \u2014 proved] Exact polynomial realization.** Fix the line parameterizations and let $S_L\u2286F_p$ be the retained parameters on $L$. Set $f=0$ and\n\n$P_L(t)=prod_{a\u2208S_L}(t-a)$.\n\nThen $P_L(t)=f(ell_L(t))$ exactly on the retained incidences. Its degree is $|S_L|\u22642\u03b4p<p$. The line-degree bounds give $\u03b1\u2208[\u03b4/2,2\u03b4]$, and $p/(64\u03b4)\u2265p/(128\u03b1)$. Since the global polynomial $Q=0$ agrees everywhere, this is only an obstruction to incidence-only sparsification.\n\n**[P12 \u2014 proved] Local agreement in the positive-degree adversary.** Before sampling $f$, fix $d+1$ points $S_L$ on every line. Sample $f$ independently and uniformly, and let $P_L$ be the unique degree-at-most-$d$ interpolant through $f|_{S_L}$. The selected points always agree. At every other point of $L$, $P_L(x)$ is determined by $f|_{S_L}$ and is independent of $f(x)$, so\n\n$E[Acc]=(d+1)/p+(p-d-1)/p^2\u2264(d+2)/p$.\n\nEvery outcome has $Acc\u2265(d+1)/p$, while Markov gives $Pr[Acc>4(d+2)/p]\u22641/4$.\n\n**[P13 \u2014 proved] Global union bound.** There are $p^K$ formal total-degree-at-most-$d$ polynomials, where $K=(d+1)(d+2)/2$. For fixed $Q$, its number of agreements with random $f$ is $Bin(p^2,1/p)$ with mean $p$. If $T\u2264p^2$, Chernoff and a union bound give\n\n$Pr[exists Q: agreements(Q)\u2265T]\u2264p^K(ep/T)^T\u2264p^K(e/4)^T\u2264p^{-(4log4-5)K}<1/4$.\n\nIf $T>p^2$, the event is empty. Thus with positive probability the Markov-good event in [P12] and this global-good event occur simultaneously, proving Proposition D.\n\n**[P14 \u2014 proved] Sharp-scale specialization.** For $d=floor(sqrt(p/log p))$, one has $K log p=O(p)$ and hence $T=O(p)$. In fact $T\u226413p$ for all sufficiently large $p$. Therefore $Agr_d\u226413/p$, while $Acc\u2265(d+1)/p$, so the ratio is at most $13/(d+1)\u21920$.\n\n**[P15 \u2014 conditional] What sparsification alone could buy.** Suppose a replacement for Lemma 3.2 gave $|R|=O(p/\u03b5^\u03b3)$ while retaining the same coefficient-count and root-budget steps. Then\n\n$D^2\u2273dp/\u03b5^\u03b3$ and $D\u2272\u03b5p$\n\nimply $d/p\u2272\u03b5^{2+\u03b3}$, or $\u03b5\u2273\u03c1^{1/(2+\u03b3)}$. Thus $|R|=O(p)$ gives exponent $1/2$, not exponent near one. Near-linear soundness needs a family near the geometric scale $\u0398(\u03b5p)$ or a different interpolation/propagation mechanism. Theorem C shows that density and minimum degree alone cannot even provide $O(p)$ on all accepted graphs.\n\n## Exponent Ledger\n\n| Stage | Input | Loss | Output |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Pruning | $\u03b5$ | absolute constants | edge measure $\u2265\u03b5/4$, $k_0=\u0398(\u03b5p)$ |\n| Sparsification | $k_0=\u0398(\u03b5p)$ | one factor $\u03b5^{-1}$ | $|R|=O(p/\u03b5)$ |\n| Interpolation | $dim(V_D)=\u03a9(D^3/d)$, $O(|R|D)$ constraints | square root | $D=O(sqrt(dp/\u03b5)+1)$ |\n| Root extension | $D<k_0/2$ | compare with $\u03b5p$ | $\u03b5^3\u2273d/p$ |\n| Trivial-line deletion | $D/p\u2264O(\u03b5)$ | compare with $\u03b5p$ | $\u03b5^3\u2273d/p$ |\n| Degenerate-line deletion | $D^2/p^2\u2264O(\u03b5)$ | quadratic term | $\u03b5^2\u2273d/p$ only |\n| Root lifting | $D<k_1/2$ | compare with $\u03b5p$ | $\u03b5^3\u2273d/p$ |\n| Formal-degree safety | $D<p$ | none beyond degree | $\u03b5\u2273d/p$ only |\n| Chernoff and mixing | $\u03b5p$, $p^{-1/2}$ | logarithmic/field-size conditions | no new $d/p$ power |\n| Propagation | one $G_1$ component | factor $1/8000$ | global agreement $\u2265\u03b5/8000$ |\n| Hypothetical $|R|=O(p/\u03b5^\u03b3)$ | same algebra | architecture conversion | exponent $1/(2+\u03b3)$ |\n| Endpoint $d=0$ | threshold zero | ratio $\u2264260/r$ | universal linear conclusion refuted |\n| Sharp-scale adversary | $\u03b5=\u0398(d/p)$ | global $O(1/p)$ | ratio $O(1/d)$ |\n\n## Counterexample Attempts\n\n1. **Degree zero \u2014 successful.** Theorem A is a genuine counterexample to the literal target, not merely to a proof technique.\n\n2. **Random prescribed interpolation \u2014 scale obstruction.** Proposition D shows that agreement of order $d/p$ cannot by itself force a constant global/local ratio. Its agreement is far below $(d/p)^{1/3}$, so it does not challenge KTZ.\n\n3. **Diffuse random incidences \u2014 proof-method obstruction.** Theorem C matches the $p/\u03b5$ reaching-family size, but its realization has the perfect decoder $Q=0$ and $d_G=\u0398(\u03b5p)$. It cannot rule out a sparsifier exploiting the stronger cubic-regime inequality $d\u226a\u03b5p$.\n\n4. **Concentrated good directions \u2014 not extremal.** If about $\u03b5p$ whole direction classes are accepted, one complete parallel class of $p$ lines reaches every point. This replaces $p/\u03b5$ by $p$ and leads to the exponent-$1/2$ ledger, so concentration is easier than the diffuse graph.\n\n5. **Near-characteristic degree \u2014 nonvacuousness failure.** At $d=p-1$, arbitrary restrictions can be interpolated on every line. After enlarging the existential KTZ constant to $C\u22651$, however, $\u03b5\u2265C(d/p)^{1/3}$ is generally impossible near $d=p$, so this does not refute the published positive-degree statement.\n\n6. **Small fields \u2014 exact checks only.** Exhaustive enumeration at $(p,d)=(2,0),(2,1),(3,0),(3,1),(3,2)$ found worst local/global ratios $4/3,4/3,7/4,3/2,9/7$, respectively. These checks expose no additional positive-degree defect and carry no asymptotic force.\n\n## Characteristic Audit\n\nFor $1\u2264d<p$, the positive-degree proof is characteristic-safe. KTZ's allowed monomials omit positive $Z^k$ with $p|k$; once $D<p$, this omission is automatic because $k\u2264D/d<p$. The minimum-weight interpolant is squarefree. Since finite fields are perfect, an irreducible factor with all ordinary partial derivatives zero would be a nontrivial $p$-th power. Lemma 2.4 then chooses an $F_p$-directional derivative outside fewer than $p$ bad subspaces; this is where the strict $D<p$ matters.\n\nRevision 1 uses no discriminant and assumes no absolute irreducibility. Coprimality of $B$ and its directional derivative is sufficient for the resultant count. The factorization $A=HB$ is exact; cancellation is in $F_p[t]$, not pointwise division by $H$. After derivative-degenerate incidences are removed, $B_Z(x,f(x))$ is a nonzero field element. Newton\u2013Hensel lifting divides only by this scalar and uses no factorials. All interpolation uses ordinary coefficient constraints and distinct roots, not hidden multiplicities.\n\nAt $d=0$, by contrast, the weighted $Z$-degree is zero, Lemma 2.2's dimension bound divides by $d$, and the minimum-weight argument loses positivity. Theorem A shows that this is a genuine theorem-level defect rather than harmless notation.\n\n## Limitations\n\n- No exponent stronger than $1/3$ is proved for the corrected regime $1\u2264d<p$; therefore `benchmark_improved=false`.\n- The endpoint counterexample corrects the literal campaign statement. It should not be presented as refuting KTZ if that paper's intended convention is $d\u22651$.\n- The reaching-set obstruction applies to arguments using only incidence density and minimum degree. Its polynomial realization has a perfect global decoder and lies at $d=\u0398(\u03b5p)$, not in the cubic regime.\n- Proposition D confirms the $d/p$ scale only up to constants; it does not rule out a theorem triggered at every sufficiently large constant multiple of $d/p$.\n- Revision 1 does not publish an explicit valid threshold constant $C$, so this audit preserves its existential constant rather than importing the old cached value.\n- Finite enumeration was used only for falsification. It cannot establish asymptotic soundness.\n- No claim is made about dimensions above two, extension fields, descent, or dimension bootstrapping.",
./research_state/campaign-10-ultra/superseded/pre-d-101/submissions/researcher-0001/note.md:35:Section 6 supplies $c=1/8000$ in the bivariate proof. Revision 1 leaves $C$ existential. The ignored local cache `.cache/references/ktz.pdf` is the earlier 17-page version, SHA-256 prefix `f8c5b145`, and must not be used to attribute its explicit $C=10^{40}$ assertion to Revision 1.

Improving only |R| to O(p) reaches exponent 1/2, not the near-linear target.","If multiplicity interpolation is attempted, explicitly count every multiplicity constraint and use Hasse derivatives so that small characteristic remains valid."],"note_markdown":"# The zero-degree endpoint is false, and the KTZ cubic loss is a sparsifier–interpolation–root-budget bottleneck\n\n## Abstract\n\nFor the affine line-versus-point test on $F_p^2$, the literal parameter range $0≤d<p$ is unsound at $d=0$. For every integer $r≥2$ and every prime\n\n$p≥p_0(r):=max\\{30,r(r-1),8r!\\}$,\n\nthere is a table $f:F_p^2→F_p$ and a constant polynomial on every line such that the local agreement $ε$ is at least $r/(130p)$, whereas every global constant agrees with $f$ on at most $2/p$ of the plane. Thus the global/local ratio is at most $260/r$, and no absolute $Ω(ε)$ conclusion holds at the zero threshold $C(0)^{1/3}$.\n\nFor the corrected regime $1≤d<p$, I find no flaw in the bivariate Kominers–Thaler–Zheng proof and no improved exponent. Its cubic loss is exact: sparsification gives $|R|=O(p/ε)$; weighted interpolation gives $D^2≈d|R|$; extension, deletion, and lifting independently require $D=O(εp)$. Hence $ε^3≳d/p$. This is my exponent reconstruction, not a separately quoted KTZ theorem.\n\nI also prove two positive-degree obstructions. First, density and minimum degree alone may require $Ω(p/ε)$ reaching lines, so the KTZ sparsifier scale cannot be improved by a purely incidence-theoretic lemma. Second, a random interpolation construction has local agreement $Θ(d/p)$ but vanishing global/local ratio along $d≈sqrt(p/log p)$, confirming that $d/p$ is the correct long-term scale up to constants.\n\n## Test and Notation\n\nFix a prime $p$, dimension $m=2$, and $0≤d<p$. Let $L$ be the set of the $p(p+1)$ geometric affine lines in $F_p^2$. Fix an affine bijection $ell_L:F_p→L$ for each line. A line table assigns $P_L∈F_p[t]$ with $deg(P_L)≤d$.\n\nThe local agreement is\n\n$Acc(f,P):=Pr_{L∈L,\\ t∈F_p}[P_L(t)=f(ell_L(t))]=:ε$.\n\nEvery line has $p$ points and every point lies on $p+1$ lines, so uniform line followed by uniform point is exactly uniform sampling from incident point-line pairs. The global agreement is\n\n$Agr_d(f):=max_{Q∈F_p[X,Y],\\ totaldeg(Q)≤d} Pr_x[Q(x)=f(x)]$.\n\nWrite $ρ=d/p$. Because $0<ρ<1$ when $d>0$, a larger threshold exponent is stronger.\n\n## Prior Results\n\nThe source-stated result is [Kominers–Thaler–Zheng, ECCC TR26-147, Revision 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/147/revision/1/download); the [ECCC record](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/147/) dates Revision 1 to August 16, 2026. Specializing Theorem 1.1 to $q=p$ and $m=2$, it states that absolute constants $C,c>0$ exist such that\n\n$ε≥C(d/p)^{1/3}$ implies $Agr_d(f)≥cε$.\n\nSection 6 supplies $c=1/8000$ in the bivariate proof. Revision 1 leaves $C$ existential. The ignored local cache `.cache/references/ktz.pdf` is the earlier 17-page version, SHA-256 prefix `f8c5b145`, and must not be used to attribute its explicit $C=10^{40}$ assertion to Revision 1.\n\nThe exact dependency map is KTZ Lemma 2.1 for incidence mixing; Lemmas 2.2–2.4 for characteristic-compatible interpolation, squarefreeness, and a coprime directional derivative; Lemmas 2.5–2.7 for line counting, the resultant bound, and simple-root lifting; Lemmas 3.1–3.2 for pruning and sparsification; Lemmas 4.1–4.3 for the explainer; Lemmas 5.1–5.2 for removal of degenerate roots; and Lemmas 6.1–6.3 for lifting and propagation. KTZ's Lemma 2.7 is traced there to [HKSS, Lemma 2.10](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.12752); Lemma 6.1 is essentially the corresponding HKSS local lifting step. No Arora–Sudan black box is used in this audit.\n\nThe corpus contains no prior mathematical submission or verifier audit: all eight leaderboard files are `[]`, neither campaign database contains submission or audit tables, and no submission or review directory exists. Thus no campaign-verified result can be imported.\n\n## Theorem\n\n### Theorem A — zero-degree endpoint counterexample\n\nFor every integer $r≥2$ and every prime $p≥p_0(r)$, there exist $f:F_p^2→F_p$ and constant line polynomials $P_L$ such that\n\n$Acc(f,P)≥r/(130p)$ and $Agr_0(f)≤2/p$.\n\nConsequently, for every proposed absolute $c>0$, choosing $r>260/c$ gives $Agr_0(f)<c Acc(f,P)$. Since $C(d/p)^{1/3}=0$ at $d=0$, the theorem requested over the literal range $0≤d<p$ is false.\n\n### Proposition B — exact positive-degree cubic bottleneck\n\nAssume $1≤d<p$ and let $ε=Acc(f,P)$. KTZ constructs\n\n$k_0=floor(εp/100)$, $100p≤r:=|R|≤6400p/ε$,\n\n$D=16(floor(sqrt(dr))+1)≤1280(sqrt(dp/ε)+1)$,\n\nand $k_1=floor(εp/1000)$.\n\nThe three requirements $D<k_0/2$, $D/p=O(ε)$, and $D<k_1/2$ all reduce to $ε^3≳d/p$. The exceptional-line term $D^2/[p(p+1)]$ asks only $ε^2≳d/p$. Once these inequalities hold, KTZ obtains $Agr_d(f)≥ε/8000$.\n\n### Theorem C — incidence-only reaching-set obstruction\n\nLet $0<δ≤1/4$ and let $p$ be prime with\n\n$δ^2p≥64 log(256ep)$.\n\nThere exists an accepted-incidence subgraph $G$ of the affine plane such that every point and line has degree at least $δp/2$, every line has degree at most $2δp$, yet no set $R$ of at most $p/(64δ)$ lines satisfies\n\n$cov_R(L):=|\\{x∈L:(L,x)∈E(G),\\text{ and some }M∈R\\text{ has }(M,x)∈E(G)\\}|>δp/4$\n\nfor every $L$ outside $R$.\n\nMoreover, $G$ is exactly realizable as a line-versus-point acceptance graph with $f=0$ and degree\n\n$d_G:=max_L deg_G(L)≤2δp<p$.\n\nIf its local agreement is $α$, then $α∈[δ/2,2δ]$, and every such reaching family has size greater than $p/(128α)$.\n\n### Proposition D — a positive-degree sharp-scale adversary\n\nFor every prime $p≥3$ and $1≤d<p$, put\n\n$K=(d+1)(d+2)/2$, $T=ceil(4(p+K log p))$.\n\nThere exist $f$ and degree-$d$ line polynomials satisfying\n\n$(d+1)/p≤Acc(f,P)≤4(d+2)/p$\n\nand\n\n$Agr_d(f)≤T/p^2$.\n\nAlong $d=floor(sqrt(p/log p))$, this gives $Agr_d(f)=O(1/p)$ and $Agr_d(f)/Acc(f,P)=O(1/d)→0$.\n\n## Proof or Conditional Proof\n\n**[P1 — proved] Sampling and source specialization.** There are $p(p+1)$ affine lines, $p$ points per line, and $p+1$ lines through each point. Hence both descriptions of the sampling distribution assign mass $1/[p^2(p+1)]$ to every incident pair. KTZ Theorem 1.1 therefore specializes without a sampling change.\n\n**[P2 — proved] KTZ sparsifier scale.** KTZ Lemma 3.1 produces an accepted graph $G_0$ of edge measure at least $ε/4$ and minimum degree $k_0=floor(εp/100)$. Lemma 3.2 chooses a line family $R$ with normalized line measure between $100/p$ and $3200/(εp)$ and covers more than $k_0/2$ accepted points of every retained line. Since the line space has $p(p+1)$ elements, this gives $100p≤|R|≤6400p/ε$.\n\n**[P3 — proved] Weighted interpolation scale.** KTZ gives $dim(V_D)≥D^3/(64d)$ for monomials of weight $(1,1,d)$. On a selected line, $A(ell_L(t),P_L(t))$ has degree at most $D$, so its formal vanishing imposes at most $D+1$ linear constraints. A nonzero interpolant therefore requires $D^3/d$ to dominate $|R|D$, or $D^2≳d|R|$. The source chooses $D=16(floor(sqrt(d|R|))+1)$, yielding the displayed bound. This coefficient count is an exponent reconstruction from KTZ's parameters.\n\n**[P4 — proved] The cubic coupling.** Dividing the displayed upper bound by $εp$ gives\n\n$D/(εp)≤1280(sqrt(ρ/ε^3)+1/(εp))$.\n\nLemma 4.3 needs $D<k_0/2$ to turn more than $D$ distinct covered roots on a line into a formal identity. Section 5 loses normalized incidence mass\n\n$D/p+D^2/[p(p+1)]$,\n\nwhose first term must be $O(ε)$. Lemma 6.1 again needs $D<k_1/2$ to obtain more than $D$ incident line roots. Each dominant requirement therefore asks $ρ/ε^3=O(1)$, namely $ε≳ρ^{1/3}$. By contrast, the quadratic deletion term is $O(ρ/ε)$ and asks only $ρ=O(ε^2)$.\n\n**[P5 — proved] Probability and constant-loss audit.** In the source bookkeeping, popularity averaging charges at most $ε/2$ to low-popularity incidences; the first degree pruning charges less than $ε/50$ and retains at least $ε/4$. Lemma 3.2 uses a Chernoff bound for each line's coverage, a union bound over $p(p+1)$ lines, and a second-moment/Chebyshev bound for $|R|$; its parameter lemma ensures $εp≫log p$. Section 5 charges exactly $D/p+D^2/[p(p+1)]≤ε/16$. The second pruning charges less than $ε/16$ and leaves edge measure at least $ε/8$. Lemma 2.1 is the sole incidence Cauchy–Schwarz loss, with normalized error at most $sqrt(μ(X)μ(T)/(p+1))$; the imposed $p^{-1/2}≤ε/8000$ yields component measures at least $ε/8000$. There is no additional Markov loss in KTZ. Thus no suppressed power of $ρ$ occurs after [P4].\n\n**[P6 — proved] A good line at d=0.** Choose $f(x)$ independently and uniformly from $F_p$. On a fixed line let $N_a$ be the occupancy of color $a$ and let $Y=sum_a 1[N_a=r]$. With\n\n$a_0=Pr[N_a=r]=binom(p,r)p^{-r}(1-1/p)^{p-r}$,\n\nthe assumptions on $p$ give\n\n$prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(1-i/p)≥1-r(r-1)/(2p)≥1/2$\n\nand $(1-1/p)^{p-r}≥(1-1/p)^p≥1/4$. Hence $a_0≥1/(8r!)$ and $E[Y]=pa_0≥1$. For distinct colors,\n\n$Pr[N_a=N_b=r]≤1/(r!)^2≤64a_0^2$.\n\nTherefore $E[Y^2]≤E[Y]+64E[Y]^2≤65E[Y]^2$. The second-moment inequality gives $Pr[Y>0]≥1/65$.\n\n**[P7 — proved] Simultaneous global balance.** Let $g(f)$ be the fraction of lines with $Y>0$, so $E[g]≥1/65$. For a fixed color, its total occupancy in the plane is $Bin(p^2,1/p)$ with mean $p$. Chernoff and a union bound over all $p$ colors give\n\n$Pr[max_a |f^{-1}(a)|>2p]≤p(e/4)^p≤pe^{-p/3}≤1/130$.\n\nIf $H$ is the complementary event, then $E[g1_H]≥1/65-1/130=1/130$. Thus some $f$ satisfies $H$ and $g(f)≥1/130$. On each good line choose a color occurring exactly $r$ times and use it as the constant $P_L$; label other lines arbitrarily. This gives $Acc≥r/(130p)$, while $H$ gives $Agr_0≤2/p$. Independence between different lines was never assumed.\n\n**[P8 — refuted] Universal soundness over 0≤d<p.** If an absolute conclusion $Agr_0≥c Acc$ held, [P7] would imply $c≤260/r$ for every $r$. Choosing $r>260/c$ is a contradiction. The threshold constant $C$ cannot repair this because its threshold is identically zero at $d=0$.\n\n**[P9 — proved] Random incidence graph.** Retain every affine point-line incidence independently with probability $δ$. Chernoff bounds and a union bound over fewer than $3p^2$ point and line vertices show, with probability greater than $3/4$ under the stated hypothesis, that every point and line has degree at least $δp/2$ and every line has degree at most $2δp$.\n\n**[P10 — proved] No small reaching family.** Fix $R$ with $r≤r_0=floor(p/(64δ))$. Some parallel class contains at most $r/(p+1)$ lines of $R$, leaving at least $p/2$ test lines outside $R$. For such a line $L$, let $n_x$ count lines of $R$ through $x∈L$. Then $sum_x n_x≤r$. The coverage indicators at distinct $x$ are independent and\n\n$E[cov_R(L)]≤δ sum_x[1-(1-δ)^{n_x}]≤δ^2r≤δp/64$.\n\nConsequently\n\n$Pr[cov_R(L)>δp/4]≤(e/16)^{δp/4}$.\n\nFor distinct parallel test lines the relevant incidence variables are disjoint, so the probability that this fixed $R$ reaches all of them is at most $exp(-δp^2/32)$. The number of sets of at most $r_0$ lines is at most\n\n$exp((p/(64δ))log(256ep))≤exp(δp^2/4096)$.\n\nA union bound gives probability less than $1/4$ that any small $R$ succeeds. Intersecting with [P9] proves Theorem C.\n\n**[P11 — proved] Exact polynomial realization.** Fix the line parameterizations and let $S_L⊆F_p$ be the retained parameters on $L$. Set $f=0$ and\n\n$P_L(t)=prod_{a∈S_L}(t-a)$.\n\nThen $P_L(t)=f(ell_L(t))$ exactly on the retained incidences. Its degree is $|S_L|≤2δp<p$. The line-degree bounds give $α∈[δ/2,2δ]$, and $p/(64δ)≥p/(128α)$. Since the global polynomial $Q=0$ agrees everywhere, this is only an obstruction to incidence-only sparsification.\n\n**[P12 — proved] Local agreement in the positive-degree adversary.** Before sampling $f$, fix $d+1$ points $S_L$ on every line. Sample $f$ independently and uniformly, and let $P_L$ be the unique degree-at-most-$d$ interpolant through $f|_{S_L}$. The selected points always agree. At every other point of $L$, $P_L(x)$ is determined by $f|_{S_L}$ and is independent of $f(x)$, so\n\n$E[Acc]=(d+1)/p+(p-d-1)/p^2≤(d+2)/p$.\n\nEvery outcome has $Acc≥(d+1)/p$, while Markov gives $Pr[Acc>4(d+2)/p]≤1/4$.\n\n**[P13 — proved] Global union bound.** There are $p^K$ formal total-degree-at-most-$d$ polynomials, where $K=(d+1)(d+2)/2$. For fixed $Q$, its number of agreements with random $f$ is $Bin(p^2,1/p)$ with mean $p$. If $T≤p^2$, Chernoff and a union bound give\n\n$Pr[exists Q: agreements(Q)≥T]≤p^K(ep/T)^T≤p^K(e/4)^T≤p^{-(4log4-5)K}<1/4$.\n\nIf $T>p^2$, the event is empty. Thus with positive probability the Markov-good event in [P12] and this global-good event occur simultaneously, proving Proposition D.\n\n**[P14 — proved] Sharp-scale specialization.** For $d=floor(sqrt(p/log p))$, one has $K log p=O(p)$ and hence $T=O(p)$. In fact $T≤13p$ for all sufficiently large $p$. Therefore $Agr_d≤13/p$, while $Acc≥(d+1)/p$, so the ratio is at most $13/(d+1)→0$.\n\n**[P15 — conditional] What sparsification alone could buy.** Suppose a replacement for Lemma 3.2 gave $|R|=O(p/ε^γ)$ while retaining the same coefficient-count and root-budget steps. Then\n\n$D^2≳dp/ε^γ$ and $D≲εp$\n\nimply $d/p≲ε^{2+γ}$, or $ε≳ρ^{1/(2+γ)}$. Thus $|R|=O(p)$ gives exponent $1/2$, not exponent near one. Near-linear soundness needs a family near the geometric scale $Θ(εp)$ or a different interpolation/propagation mechanism. Theorem C shows that density and minimum degree alone cannot even provide $O(p)$ on all accepted graphs.\n\n## Exponent Ledger\n\n| Stage | Input | Loss | Output |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Pruning | $ε$ | absolute constants | edge measure $≥ε/4$, $k_0=Θ(εp)$ |\n| Sparsification | $k_0=Θ(εp)$ | one factor $ε^{-1}$ | $|R|=O(p/ε)$ |\n| Interpolation | $dim(V_D)=Ω(D^3/d)$, $O(|R|D)$ constraints | square root | $D=O(sqrt(dp/ε)+1)$ |\n| Root extension | $D<k_0/2$ | compare with $εp$ | $ε^3≳d/p$ |\n| Trivial-line deletion | $D/p≤O(ε)$ | compare with $εp$ | $ε^3≳d/p$ |\n| Degenerate-line deletion | $D^2/p^2≤O(ε)$ | quadratic term | $ε^2≳d/p$ only |\n| Root lifting | $D<k_1/2$ | compare with $εp$ | $ε^3≳d/p$ |\n| Formal-degree safety | $D<p$ | none beyond degree | $ε≳d/p$ only |\n| Chernoff and mixing | $εp$, $p^{-1/2}$ | logarithmic/field-size conditions | no new $d/p$ power |\n| Propagation | one $G_1$ component | factor $1/8000$ | global agreement $≥ε/8000$ |\n| Hypothetical $|R|=O(p/ε^γ)$ | same algebra | architecture conversion | exponent $1/(2+γ)$ |\n| Endpoint $d=0$ | threshold zero | ratio $≤260/r$ | universal linear conclusion refuted |\n| Sharp-scale adversary | $ε=Θ(d/p)$ | global $O(1/p)$ | ratio $O(1/d)$ |\n\n## Counterexample Attempts\n\n1. **Degree zero — successful.** Theorem A is a genuine counterexample to the literal target, not merely to a proof technique.\n\n2. **Random prescribed interpolation — scale obstruction.** Proposition D shows that agreement of order $d/p$ cannot by itself force a constant global/local ratio. Its agreement is far below $(d/p)^{1/3}$, so it does not challenge KTZ.\n\n3. **Diffuse random incidences — proof-method obstruction.** Theorem C matches the $p/ε$ reaching-family size, but its realization has the perfect decoder $Q=0$ and $d_G=Θ(εp)$. It cannot rule out a sparsifier exploiting the stronger cubic-regime inequality $d≪εp$.\n\n4. **Concentrated good directions — not extremal.** If about $εp$ whole direction classes are accepted, one complete parallel class of $p$ lines reaches every point. This replaces $p/ε$ by $p$ and leads to the exponent-$1/2$ ledger, so concentration is easier than the diffuse graph.\n\n5. **Near-characteristic degree — nonvacuousness failure.** At $d=p-1$, arbitrary restrictions can be interpolated on every line. After enlarging the existential KTZ constant to $C≥1$, however, $ε≥C(d/p)^{1/3}$ is generally impossible near $d=p$, so this does not refute the published positive-degree statement.\n\n6. **Small fields — exact checks only.** Exhaustive enumeration at $(p,d)=(2,0),(2,1),(3,0),(3,1),(3,2)$ found worst local/global ratios $4/3,4/3,7/4,3/2,9/7$, respectively. These checks expose no additional positive-degree defect and carry no asymptotic force.\n\n## Characteristic Audit\n\nFor $1≤d<p$, the positive-degree proof is characteristic-safe. KTZ's allowed monomials omit positive $Z^k$ with $p|k$; once $D<p$, this omission is automatic because $k≤D/d<p$. The minimum-weight interpolant is squarefree. Since finite fields are perfect, an irreducible factor with all ordinary partial derivatives zero would be a nontrivial $p$-th power. Lemma 2.4 then chooses an $F_p$-directional derivative outside fewer than $p$ bad subspaces; this is where the strict $D<p$ matters.\n\nRevision 1 uses no discriminant and assumes no absolute irreducibility. Coprimality of $B$ and its directional derivative is sufficient for the resultant count. The factorization $A=HB$ is exact; cancellation is in $F_p[t]$, not pointwise division by $H$. After derivative-degenerate incidences are removed, $B_Z(x,f(x))$ is a nonzero field element. Newton–Hensel lifting divides only by this scalar and uses no factorials. All interpolation uses ordinary coefficient constraints and distinct roots, not hidden multiplicities.\n\nAt $d=0$, by contrast, the weighted $Z$-degree is zero, Lemma 2.2's dimension bound divides by $d$, and the minimum-weight argument loses positivity. Theorem A shows that this is a genuine theorem-level defect rather than harmless notation.\n\n## Limitations\n\n- No exponent stronger than $1/3$ is proved for the corrected regime $1≤d<p$; therefore `benchmark_improved=false`.\n- The endpoint counterexample corrects the literal campaign statement. It should not be presented as refuting KTZ if that paper's intended convention is $d≥1$.\n- The reaching-set obstruction applies to arguments using only incidence density and minimum degree. Its polynomial realization has a perfect global decoder and lies at $d=Θ(εp)$, not in the cubic regime.\n- Proposition D confirms the $d/p$ scale only up to constants; it does not rule out a theorem triggered at every sufficiently large constant multiple of $d/p$.\n- Revision 1 does not publish an explicit valid threshold constant $C$, so this audit preserves its existential constant rather than importing the old cached value.\n- Finite enumeration was used only for falsification. It cannot establish asymptotic soundness.\n- No claim is made about dimensions above two, extension fields, descent, or dimension bootstrapping." ,"obstructions":["The literal inclusion of d=0 makes the benchmark false because its threshold is zero while constant line labels can have much larger local than global agreement.","Within the KTZ architecture, |R|=O(p/ε), D^2≳d|R|, and D≲εp algebraically force ε^3≳d/p.","A density/minimum-degree-only reaching lemma cannot improve |R|=Θ(p/ε) in general; the random accepted-incidence graph proves a matching lower bound up to constants.","Even an O(p) reaching family would yield only exponent 1/2 under the same interpolation and root-budget steps.","The positive-degree interpolation adversary shows that Θ(d/p) is a necessary scale up to constants, but it does not determine the optimal threshold constant.","The official Revision 1 and the cached original differ: Revision 1 does not state the cached version's explicit C=10^40."],"parameter_regime":"Dimension m=2; prime p; literal campaign range 0≤d<p. Let p be prime, let 101≤d<p, fix m=2, and use uniform affine point-line sampling. There exist f:F_p²→F_p and degree-exactly-d line labels such that ε=d/p, every affine line has exactly d accepted points, every point has accepted degree d or d+1, exactly pd points have degree d+1, and every line supports acceptance. For every θ∈(0,1], the minimum total nonnegative line weight that gives every accepted incidence θ capped coverage through other accepted lines lies between θp(p+1)/(d+1) and θp(p+1)/(d−1); hence every integral such family has size Ω(p/ε). Nevertheless Q=0 has global agreement one. Moreover, for every valid table of agreement ε, the probability Γ₂ that two uniformly chosen distinct lines through a uniform point both accept there satisfies Γ₂=ε²−ε(1−ε)/p+Var_x(deg_acc(x))/[p(p+1)], and the lower bound is attained by accepting exactly k direction classes, ε=k/(p+1).

# Literature and Theorem Map

This file is a navigation aid, not a substitute for the papers. Agents must cite exact theorem and lemma identifiers from the primary sources they actually use.

The active campaign extracts only the \(m=2\), prime-field content of these papers. General-dimensional lifting is standard downstream machinery and is not an active research target.

## Primary line-versus-point papers

### Arora--Sudan

Sanjeev Arora and Madhu Sudan, *Improved Low-Degree Testing and Its Applications*, Combinatorica 23 (2003), 365--426. DOI: [10.1007/s00493-003-0025-0](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00493-003-0025-0).

The foundational low-agreement analysis of the Rubinfeld--Sudan test. For this campaign, isolate its bivariate algebraic argument over \(\mathbb F_p\), including the use of Hilbert irreducibility and its field-size requirement. Its separate dimension-bootstrapping argument is historical context only.

### Harsha--Kumar--Saptharishi--Sudan (HKSS)

Prahladh Harsha, Mrinal Kumar, Ramprasad Saptharishi, and Madhu Sudan, *An Improved Line-Point Low-Degree Test*, arXiv:2311.12752. [Primary preprint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.12752).

This work replaces the black-box use of Hilbert irreducibility with a more direct bivariate factorization/interpolation analysis. Kominers--Thaler--Zheng estimate the resulting bivariate low-agreement exponent as \(1/7\); that numerical extraction should be attributed to KTZ unless independently rederived. The later lift to general dimension is routine for present purposes and is not assigned to agents.

### Kominers--Thaler--Zheng (KTZ)

Scott Duke Kominers, Justin Thaler, and Kai Zhe Zheng, *Improved Soundness for the Line--versus--Point Test*, ECCC TR26-147, revision 1 (2026). [Primary report](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/147/).

The current benchmark proves a cubic threshold \(C(d/q)^{1/3}\) over every finite field, hence in particular \(C(d/p)^{1/3}\) on \(\mathbb F_p^2\), with global agreement at least a constant times the local agreement. The campaign uses only this bivariate prime-field specialization. Submissions should identify exactly which combinatorial or algebraic step forces the exponent \(1/3\).

## Adjacent foundations

- Ronitt Rubinfeld and Madhu Sudan, *Robust Characterizations of Polynomials with Applications to Program Testing*, SIAM Journal on Computing 25 (1996). This is the test's foundational high-agreement setting.
- Ran Raz and Shmuel Safra, *A Sub-Constant Error-Probability Low-Degree Test, and a Sub-Constant Error-Probability PCP Characterization of NP*, STOC 1997. This is a plane-based comparison and must not be conflated with line-versus-point.
- Irit Dinur, Prahladh Harsha, Rakesh Venkat, and Henry Yuen, *Multiplayer Parallel Repetition for Expanding Games*, including the low-degree-test context where relevant. Use only exact statements that apply to the chosen test distribution.

## Questions the campaign should track

1. Which inequality in the KTZ bivariate argument creates the cubic threshold?
2. Is the loss algebraic, combinatorial, or caused by converting average line agreement into a structured set of points/directions?
3. Can multiplicity-sensitive interpolation, Hasse derivatives, or separability arguments reduce the loss?
4. Can a direct argument using the geometry of \(\mathbb F_p^2\) avoid a lossy intermediate lemma?
5. What examples show that a threshold below \(\Theta(d/p)\) is impossible?
6. Which statements fail in small prime characteristic, for \(d\ge p\), or when affine-line sampling is changed?

## Citation policy

- Record the paper, version/date, theorem or lemma number, hypotheses, and the exact role of every imported result.
- Mark any reconstructed exponent as a derivation rather than a quoted theorem.
- Do not cite survey prose for a decisive lemma when the primary paper is available.
- Do not include paper PDFs in Git. `scripts/fetch_references.py` can populate a local ignored cache for research use.

                                              Electronic Colloquium on Computational Complexity, Report No. 147 (2026)
                          
                                   Improved Soundness for the Line–versus–Point Test
                                       Scott Duke Kominers                    Justin Thaler               Kai Zhe Zheng
                                                                            July 2026
                                                                             Abstract
                               The line–versus–point test asks the following local-to-global question. Suppose a function
                           f: Fm →Fq is, on average over a random afÏne line L, correlated with some degree-d polynomial
                                q
                           on L. Must f then be globally correlated with a single multivariate polynomial of degree at
                           most d? Beyond being a natural combinatorial question, this test lies at the heart of low-degree
                           testing and has played a central role in the construction of PCPs. Low-degree tests and their
                           higher-dimensional variants have also contributed to results such as the 2-to-2 Games Theorem
                           and two-query PCPs with nearly optimal alphabet–soundness tradeoffs.
                               Of particular interest is the soundness of the test: the smallest expected local agreement
                           from which one can still deduce nontrivial global agreement. We revisit the line–versus–point
                           test and, building on the algebraic approach of [HKSS24], prove the following cubic threshold. If
                                                                                           d1/3
                                                                Pr    P (x) = f(x) ≥ C                ,
                                                              L,x∈L    L                       q
                           then there is a polynomial Q ∈ F [X ,...,X ] of total degree at most d such that
                                                                  q   1        m
                                                                                                          
                                                         Pr [Q(x) = f(x)] ≥ c· Pr           PL(x) = f(x) ,
                                                        x∈Fm                        L,x∈L
                                                            q
                           for absolute constants C,c > 0 and over every finite field Fq. Our proof is comparatively direct
                           and isolates the combinatorial and algebraic mechanisms responsible for the improved soundness.
                    1     Introduction
                    The Reed–Muller code is one of the basic algebraic codes in theoretical computer science. Its
                    codewords are the evaluation tables of low-degree polynomials:
                                                                          m                                            	
                                           RM (m,d):= (Q(x))                  : Q ∈ F [X ,...,X ], degQ ≤ d .
                                                q                      x∈Fq            q    1         m
                    Much of the usefulness of Reed–Muller codes comes from their strong locality. The restriction of a
                    degree-d polynomial to any afÏne line is a univariate polynomial of degree at most d, and hence a
                    Reed–Solomon codeword. Thus a global Reed–Muller codeword induces a highly structured local
                    view on every afÏne line. This observation underlies local testing and correction procedures that
                    inspect only a small part of a word of length qm.
                        One particularly important test arising from this structure is the line–versus–point test, a
                    two-query consistency test. We are given a point table f: Fm → F and, for every afÏne line L,
                                                                                                   q       q
                    a purported degree-d restriction PL. The test samples a uniformly random incident pair (x,L),
                    queries f(x) and P (x), and accepts when
                                            L
                                                                          PL(x) = f(x).
                                                                                  1
                                                                                                                                         ISSN 1433-8092 
                If the point and line tables are induced by a single degree-d polynomial, the test always accepts. Its
                soundness asks for a converse: how small can the acceptance probability be while still forcing one
                global degree-d polynomial to agree with f on a noticeable fraction of points?
                    Low-degree tests, such as the line–versus–point test and its variants, have played a central role
                in PCP constructions and hardness of approximation since the earliest algebraic PCPs [PS94, RS96,
                AS98,ALM+98,RS97,AS03]. Theirtwo-queryformisparticularly useful for constructing projection
                PCPsandLabel Cover instances, which serve as starting points for many hardness-of-approximation
                reductions [MR10]. Higher-dimensional subspace agreement tests have also been central to the series
                of works leading to the 2-to-2 Games Theorem [KMS25, DKK+25, DKK+21, KMS23] and to recent
                two-query and k-query PCPs with nearly optimal alphabet–soundness tradeoffs [MZ24, MZ26].
                More recently, the line–versus–point test has been used to construct query-efÏcient interactive oracle
                proofs of proximity for NP [MZ25].
                    Beyond these applications, the line–versus–point test is a natural local-to-global agreement
                problem in its own right. It asks whether a collection of low-degree polynomials on afÏne lines
                that is locally consistent with a point table must contain evidence of a single global low-degree
                polynomial. This question was introduced by Rubinfeld and Sudan and has been studied through
                a sequence of increasingly strong analyses [RS96, PS94, FS95, AS03, HKSS24]. It is also closely
                related to the property-testing problem for Reed–Muller codes, in which one seeks to distinguish
                low-degree polynomials from functions that are far from every such polynomial using only a small
                number of queries [AKK+05, KR06, BKS+10, HSS13]. The models and degree regimes in these
                works are not always identical, but they share the same basic theme of inferring global polynomial
                structure from local algebraic constraints.
                    Acentral quantitative question is how far into the low-agreement, or list-decoding, regime the
                line–versus–point test remains sound.
                    Polishchuk and Spielman, and subsequently Friedl and Sudan, initiated the study of the
                line–versus–point test in the low-error, or unique-decoding, regime [PS94, FS95]. Arora and
                Sudan subsequently obtained a high-error analysis when the field is polynomially larger than the
                degree [AS03]. Harsha, Kumar, Saptharishi, and Sudan gave the first high-error analysis when the
                                                                                                          Ω(1)
                field size is linear in the degree [HKSS24]. Their work showed that acceptance (d/q)          already
                forces global structure and introduced the key algebraic mechanism of constructing a low weighted-
                degree trivariate relation and extracting a bivariate polynomial through Newton–Hensel lifting. They
                state their all-dimensional result with an unspecified absolute exponent. Tracing the losses in their
                                                    1/48                                                           1/7
                proof gives the threshold ε ≳ (d/q) 4 , while their direct bivariate argument requires ε ≳ (d/q)
                and initially recovers agreement Ω(ε ).
                    Building on the analysis of [HKSS24], we introduce new combinatorial and algebraic ideas that
                improve the soundness threshold to                
                                                                   d 1/3
                                                             ε ≳   q
                and the recovered agreement to Ω(ε). We first establish these bounds in the bivariate case and then
                preserve them in arbitrary dimension.
                1.1    Our results

                in two dimensions and the field is clear from context, we instead write L for L     . For every afÏne
                                                                                                  q,2
                line L, fix once and for all an afÏne parameterization, consisting of translate a  ∈Landdirection
                                                                                                 L

                u , and write L(t) := a +tu to denote the t-th point of L. We slightly abuse notation by using L

                both for the afÏne line and, through L(·), for its fixed parameterization.
                    Our results deal with the classic line–versus–point test. In this test, we are given a point

                                                   q   1       m

                                                              x∈Fm
                                                                 q
                Mechanization. Theorem 1.1 has been formalized and machine-checked in Lean 4 over the
                Mathlib library. The bivariate theorem is verified with no assumptions beyond Lean’s logical
                                                                                              40
                foundations, with certified (deliberately slack) explicit constants C = 10       and c = 1/8000; the
                all-dimensional theorem is verified assuming exactly the two imported results, Theorem 7.2 and
                Lemma 7.1.
                1.2    Proof overview and technical contributions
                Our proof proceeds in two stages. We first establish the result in the bivariate case m = 2. This
                bivariate statement is the main lemma underlying the theorem, and its proof contains most of the
                work. We then bootstrap it to arbitrary dimension using the results of [BR25].
                Why the bivariate line case is the core. The difÏculty comes from the sparse overlap of
                line views. In tests based on planes or cubes, two local views can share an entire line or plane,
                so a consistency event compares many related values at once [RS97, BDN17, MZ23, BR25]. Two
                distinct lines, by contrast, share at most one point. Thus each accepted line–point incidence supplies
                only one scalar equality, and most of the proof is devoted to turning this sparse information into a
                                                                   3
                relation that can be propagated globally. Once the bivariate statement is established, the passage
                to higher dimensions is comparatively direct.
                   At a high level, we follow the strategy of [HKSS24]. Let G    be the bipartite graph whose two
                                                            2                acc
                sides are the afÏne lines L and the points F , with an edge (x,L) whenever PL(x) = f(x). Thus,
                                                            q
                the hypothesis that the line–versus–point test accepts with probability at least ε is exactly the
                statement that the accepted edge set Eacc has measure µ(Eacc) ≥ ε among all incidences (x,L) with
                x∈L.
                   Starting from these accepted incidences, both proofs construct a trivariate explainer polynomial
                A(X,Y,Z) and arrange that, for many lines L,
                                                       A(L(t),p (t)) ≡ 0.
                                                                L
               They then find a point b through which many of these lines pass, with PL(b) = f(b) for every such
                line. When f(b) is a simple root of the explainer at b, Newton–Hensel lifting produces a global
                bivariate polynomial Q(X,Y) whose restrictions agree with the corresponding line polynomials.
                   In the bivariate argument of [HKSS24], the global polynomial Q is obtained by lifting at one
                suitable point b, and its agreement with f is obtained from the good lines through b. Thus, the final
                agreement count is tied to one local neighborhood of b in G  , which is one quantitative limitation
                                                                          acc
                of their argument.
                   To go beyond this limitation, we observe that the polynomial lifted at b can agree with the point
                table beyond the lines through b. For this purpose, we use the informative part of the explainer A.
                More precisely, we factor
                                                A(X,Y,Z)=H(X,Y)B(X,Y,Z),
               where H is the greatest common factor of the coefÏcients of A when it is viewed as a polynomial in
                Z. The factor H can vanish identically on a line independently of the line polynomial PL, in which
                case the identity A(L(t),p (t)) ≡ 0 gives no information about p . Dividing out H removes these
                                          L                                     L
                trivial vanishing relations and leaves a common explainer B(X,Y,Z) for the line polynomials that
                remain.
                   Wethen arrange that, at every point x in the graph under consideration, the value f(x) is not a
                repeated root of
                                                        B(x,Z)∈Fq[Z].
               This makes the local lift uniquely determined. Suppose Q|       = P for a line L in the current
                subgraph, and let (x,L) be an accepted edge. Then            L      L
                                                      Q(x) = P (x) = f(x).
                                                               L
                If M is another accepted line through x, then P     and Q|    solve the same restricted explainer
                                                                 M         M
                equation and agree at the simple root f(x). The algebraic uniqueness of a lift from a simple root
                therefore gives Q|  =P (later stated in Lemma 2.7). Repeating this step along paths propagates
                                 M     M
                the same polynomial through an entire connected component of the pruned accepted-incidence
                graph. This allows us to show that Q agrees with f on a linear fraction of the points in that
                component, rather than only on the agreement sets of the lines through one point.
                1. Interpolating a sparse, but global, set of afÏne lines.        Westart by finding a subgraph
                G0, of Gacc, with edge measure Ω(ε) and minimum degree Ω(εq) on both sides, and then randomly
                choose a set of O(q/ε) from the “line side” of G . Denote this set of lines by R. Crucially, R satisfies
                                                              0
                the following two properties
                                                                4
                 • Small size: |R| = O(q/ε), so that a polynomial interpolating the graphs of {P }      can
                    have low weighted degree.                                                   M M∈R
                 • Globalness: for every line L in the dense subgraph, there are Ω(εq) points x ∈ L such that
                    (x,L) is accepted and x also lies on an accepted line M ∈ R.
               Weinterpolate a nonzero trivariate polynomial A(X,Y,Z) of weighted degree D := Oqdq such
                                                                                                    ε
               that A(M(t),p (t)) ≡ 0 for every M ∈ R. In a sense, A now “encodes” the global agreement of f
                             M
               with a low-degree polynomial, and the later steps extract this agreement out of A.
                  Weremark that the interpolation step is more efÏcient than the corresponding step in [HKSS24],
               although the gain is not simply that we interpolate asymptotically fewer lines at the final threshold.
               Weinterpolate only a family R of size O(q/ε) while preserving the original ε-scale incidence density.
               At first this seems to leave too few lines from which to recover substantial agreement. The key is
               the global property of R: every line in the dense subgraph shares Ω(εq) accepted points with lines
               in R. These shared incidences extend the interpolated relation to the entire subgraph, and the later
               propagation step converts this relation into agreement on a whole connected component.
               2. Simple roots throughout the incidence graph. Before extracting a bivariate polynomial
               from the common relation
                                                    A(L(t),p (t)) ≡ 0,
                                                             L
               we need to arrange two properties. First, the relation on each remaining line must genuinely
               constrain its line polynomial, rather than vanish for a reason independent of the Z-variable. Second,
               at every remaining point x where we hope to get global agreement, the value f(x) must be a simple
               root of the resulting polynomial in Z. The first property makes the line identities informative, while
               the second is needed to make the later lifting and propagation steps go through.
                  To obtain the first property, write
                                             A(X,Y,Z)=H(X,Y)B(X,Y,Z),
               where H is the greatest common factor of the coefÏcients of A when viewed as a polynomial in Z. If
               H(L(t)) ≡ 0, then A(L(t),p (t)) ≡ 0 for every choice of p , so the identity contains no information
                                         L                           L
               about the polynomial attached to L. We remove these lines; there are few of them because each
               contributes a distinct linear factor of H. On every other line, cancellation gives the informative
               identity
                                                    B(L(t),p (t)) ≡ 0.
                                                             L
                  We next arrange the simple-root property. Choose λ ,λ ,λ     ∈ Fq so that B and λ B +

               λ B +λ B have no common factor. Here B ,B , and B are the X,Y, and Z derivatives

               3. Componentwise continuation. We can now choose a point b in the remaining incidence
               graph and lift a degree-d bivariate polynomial Q. The lifting argument guarantees that Q agrees
               with the line polynomials on a collection of lines through b. By itself, however, this local agreement
               is not enough to obtain the desired Ω(ε) agreement with f.
                   This is another point at which we must go beyond the analysis of [HKSS24]. Rather than counting
               only the agreement contributed by the lines through the lifting point b, as is done in [HKSS24], we
               use the simple-root property arranged in Step 2 to propagate the same polynomial throughout an
               entire connected component of the incidence graph. Expansion of the point–line incidence graph
               shows that each such component is sufÏciently large, guaranteeing that the agreement of Q with f
               is Ω(ε).
                   Suppose Q| = P for one remaining line L, and let x ∈ L be an accepted incidence. Then
                              L    L
                                                    Q(x) = P (x) = f(x).
                                                             L
               For any other remaining line M through x, both Q|    and P    satisfy the same restricted relation
                                                                  M       M
                                                       B(M(t),Z)=0
               and pass through the same simple root f(x). Algebraic uniqueness of the lift from a simple root then
               yields Q|  =P . Finally, repeating this argument along paths propagates the same polynomial
                        M     M
               from the lines through b to every line and point in the connected component as desired.
               4. Higher dimensions. Finally, we bootstrap the bivariate result to arbitrary dimension. Here
               we leverage the recent plane–versus–plane theorem of Bhangale and Richelson [BR25], which has
               the optimal soundness threshold Ω(d/q). A simple conversion shows that plane–point agreement β
                                                               2                                          1/2
               induces plane–versus–plane acceptance roughly β ; thus their theorem applies once β ≳ (d/q)   .
                   The rest of the argument is relatively direct. Most afÏne planes inherit line–versus–point
               acceptance Ω(ε), so the bivariate theorem gives a degree-d polynomial on each such plane. We apply
               the Bhangale–Richelson theorem to glue these plane polynomials into one global polynomial, and
               point–plane expansion recovers Ω(ε) agreement with f.
                   The bootstrap step in [HKSS24], developed before this optimal plane–versus–plane theorem was
               available, requires a more involved intermediate correction argument and consequently incurs larger
               quantitative losses. The result of Bhangale and Richelson allows us to avoid that difÏculty and pass
               directly from the bivariate theorem to a consistent global polynomial.

                1.3    Organization
                Section 2 records notation and preliminaries. Sections 3 and 4 constructs the trivariate explainer
                polynomial, Section 5 makes its relevant roots simple, and Section 6 lifts and propagates one bivariate
                polynomial and completes the bivariate proof. Finally, Section 7 handles higher dimensions.
                2     Preliminaries
                2.1    Measure and afÏne-plane incidence
                Every finite set carries its uniform probability measure. We use µ for point, line, plane, incidence,

               2.3   Squarefree interpolants and derivatives
                                      3
               Lemma 2.3. Let S ⊆ F be finite, and suppose there is a nonzero polynomial that vanishes on S
                                      q
               and has nonzero Z-derivative. If A has minimum weighted degree among all such polynomials, then
               no nonconstant irreducible factor occurs in A with multiplicity at least two.
               Proof. Write A = PeR with e ≥ 2, P irreducible, and P ∤ R. Then PR has the same zero set and
               smaller weighted degree. If (PR) = 0, reducing P R+PR = 0 modulo P gives P | P , hence
                                               Z                Z        Z                          Z
               PZ = RZ = 0, contrary to AZ ̸= 0. Thus PR is another admissible interpolant, contradicting
               minimality.
               Lemma 2.4. Let B ∈ F [X,Y,Z] be nonzero and squarefree with degB < q. There is a vector
                                   3   q
               λ=(λ ,λ ,λ )∈F such that, for
                     X Y Z         q

               nonzero partial derivative. The vectors λ with D Pi = 0 form a proper subspace of F . There are

               s ≤ degB < q such subspaces, so choose λ outside their union. Then no P divides D B.

                                       q

               linear factors.

                3    Step 1: Preparing the Accepted Incidences for Interpolation
                The goal is to choose a small family R for interpolation without losing contact with the accepted

               6    Constructing and Propagating the Bivariate Polynomial
               Thegoal of this section is to convert the local line roots retained in G1 into a single degree-d bivariate
               polynomial. Starting from any point of a connected component, the simple-root condition first allows
               us to lift the incident line polynomials to a bivariate root Q of B. Initially, this polynomial agrees
               only with the line polynomials through the starting point. The same simple-root condition then
               allows us to propagate Q through the entire connected component, producing a single polynomial
               that agrees with all line polynomials and point values in that component. Finally, a standard
               application of the expander mixing lemma to the point–line incidence graph gives the desired Ω(ε)
               global agreement with f and proves the m = 2 case of Theorem 1.1.
                   The next lemma is essentially taken from Lemma 3.1 of [HKSS24], stated in the form needed
               here. It carries out the initial lifting step at a single point. We require a slightly different form of
               the lemma for our purposes, so we restate its proof below for completeness.
                                                              12
                                                                                           2
               Lemma6.1. Suppose B ∈ Fq[X,Y,Z] has weighted degree at most D. Let b ∈ F and α ∈ Fq satisfy
                                                                                           q
                                                B(b,α) = 0,     BZ(b,α) ̸= 0.
               Suppose U contains more than D distinct lines through b, and each L ∈ U carries a polynomial PL
               of degree at most d such that
                                              P (b) = α,     B(L(t),p (t)) ≡ 0.
                                               L                      L
               Then there is a polynomial Q ∈ Fq[X,Y] of total degree at most d such that
                                    B(X,Y,Q(X,Y))≡0          and     Q| =P       (L ∈ U).
                                                                        L    L
               Proof. Translate so that b = 0 and parameterize each line as L(t) = tu . Apply Lemma 2.7 with
                                                                                    L
               k = d to obtain Φ . For every L ∈ U, both Φ (tu ) and p (t) have degree at most d, constant
                                 d                           d   L       L
               term α, and satisfy the same equation modulo td+1. The uniqueness property in Lemma 2.7 gives
               Φ | =P .
                 d L    L
                   Set Q = Φd and R(X,Y) = B(X,Y,Q(X,Y)). Then degR ≤ D, and R(L(t)) ≡ 0 for more than
               Dlines. Hence Lemma 2.5 gives R = 0.
                   Next, we show that no connected component of G is too small. This follows from the minimum-
                                                                  1
               degree conditions in G and the point–line mixing lemma.
                                     1

                   It remains to show that a single degree-d polynomial governs all of the line and point labels in
               such a component. Starting from one point, Lemma 6.1 constructs the polynomial on the incident
               lines, and simple-root uniqueness propagates it through the component.
               Lemma 6.3. For every connected component (X′,T′) of G1, there is a polynomial Q ∈ Fq[X,Y] of
               total degree at most d such that Q(x) = f(x) for all x ∈ X′ and Q| = P  for all L ∈ T′.
                                                                                L    L
               Proof. Choose b ∈ X′ arbitrarily. By Lemmas 5.2 and 6.1, there is a degree-d polynomial Q with
               B(X,Y,Q(X,Y))≡0 and Q| =P for every line adjacent to b.
                                           L     L
                   Suppose Q| =P for a line already reached, and let (x,L) ∈ E . Then Q(x) = f(x). If M is
                              L    L                                             1
               another line adjacent to x, parameterize it with M(0) = x. The polynomials p (t) and Q(M(t))

               Since BZ(x,f(x)) ̸= 0, the uniqueness case in the one-variable version of Lemma 2.7 gives Q|M = PM.
               Connectivity propagates this identity to every line of T′, and gives Q(x) = f(x) for every x ∈ X′.

                    Weare now able to prove our main theorem in the dimension m = 2 case.
                Proof of Theorem 1.1. Choose the absolute constant in Theorem 1.1 larger than all constants
                required in Lemmas 3.2 and 4.1. Construct G0 using Lemma 3.1. Choose the line set R by
                Lemma 3.2, construct the minimum interpolating polynomial by Lemma 4.2, and use Lemma 4.3 to
                extend its vanishing identity to every line of G0. The parameter regime of Lemma 4.1 is used twice,
                at two different scales: this extension needs D < k0/2, while the lifting and propagation below need
                the stronger D < k /2 after the second round of pruning.
                                    1
                    Pass from A to B, remove the exceptional lines and incidences described in Section 5, and
                prune to the subgraph G1 using Lemma 5.2. Choose any connected component (X′,T′) of G1. By
                Lemma 6.3, one degree-d polynomial Q agrees with f at every point of X′. By Lemma 6.2,
                                                                         k       ε

                Thus the theorem holds with c = 1/8000.

                                               p                 PP PP

                                            PP                   m,q

                Proof. Complete the table outside G by partitioning P      \G into q classes whose sizes differ by

                If Q is nonconstant, then Q can agree with at most a 2d/q fraction of the planes in P  \G by the
                                                                                                    m,q
                Schwartz–Zippel lemma. Hence, for every global degree-d polynomial Q, the measure of planes on
               which it can agree with the table T coming from P      \G is at most 2d/q.
                                                                   m,q
                   The completed table passes the plane–versus–point test with probability at least β/2. We show
                that it passes the plane–versus–plane test with probability Ω(β2). Choose two random distinct
                planes meeting in a line and then a random point on that line. The probability that both plane
                polynomials equal f at this point is at least β2/4 − 1/(q2 + q). If their restrictions to the line are
                distinct, they agree at no more than d points. Hence
                                            Acc     (T) ≥ β2 − d −     1    =Ω(β2).
                                                PVP        4    q    q2 +q
                For sufÏciently large CPP, Theorem 7.2 therefore gives a global polynomial Q agreeing with T on
                a plane set M of measure Ω(β2). Since only O(d/q) = o(β2) of these matches can come from the
                completion, M∩G still has measure Ω(β2).
                   Let XQ = {x : Q(x) = f(x)}. Every plane in M∩G contains at least a β-fraction of points from
                XQ. Applying Lemma 7.1(ii) gives
                                                     β ≤µ(XQ)+O 1 .
                                                                       qβ
               The error is at most β/2 once CPP is sufÏciently large, which proves the claim.

name: control-in-app-browser
description: "Control the in-app Browser for opening, navigating, inspecting visible or interactive page state, clicking, typing, screenshots, and local web testing. It can have existing signed-in sessions. For semantic operations on linked resources, prefer a purpose-built connector, API, or CLI when available."
